- **Student-detail Rent tab now follows the student’s class-scoped rent flag (2026-09-08)** — It no longer depends on the deprecated hardcoded global-rent context flag.

- **Hall-pass verification is now visibly usable on first setup (2026-09-08)** — The teacher page labels the first token action as **Generate QR**, renders the verification URL as an absolute link, and displays a QR image whenever a token exists.

- **A grant invalidated by reversal is `REVOKED`, distinguished by cause rather than by state (2026-09-07)** — SPEC-OPS-001 §3.3 previously asked for two downstream states, `VOID` and `INVALIDATED_BY_REVERSAL`, kept semantically apart. The entitlement vocabulary has three terminal states — `CONSUMED`, `EXPIRED`, `REVOKED` — fixed by a partial unique index and a `String(20)` column, and no code ever wrote the fourth. §3.3 now settles it the other way: a capability withdrawn by reversal reports as `REVOKED`, the same state a directly authorized grant void writes.

  That is the honest reading rather than the convenient one. The state answers what the student may now do, and the answer is identical in both cases — nothing. Splitting it would have made every consumer of entitlement status carry a distinction that changes no decision it makes, and would have needed a migration on the terminal index to say so. INV-OPS-002 keeps the requirement where it belongs: the *cause* must stay recoverable, so an invalidation propagated by a reversal names the transaction it followed from. `transaction_void_feat._void_purchase` records `reversal_propagated` and `reversed_transaction_id` in the event payload, replacing a `transaction_void` reason that named the act CTH no longer performs on money.

- **SPEC-STORE-001 §V.A said bundling was forbidden on the types that support it (2026-09-07)** — The type-specific rules carried "Cannot be bundled or part of collective goal" under DELAYED_USE and HALL_PASS, which contradicted §III (where `bundle_quantity`'s only stated exclusion is the collective goal), §V.B.1, and §V.C.4 ("bundle_quantity: If set, must be > 1"). Read literally, `bundle_quantity` could never legally be set on anything and those three clauses were dead text. The document now states the rule once — **only DELAYED_USE and HALL_PASS may be bundled**, because a bundle grants multiple unexercised units and only those two types can hold them — which is what `store_service._BUNDLEABLE_ITEM_TYPES` has always enforced. IMMEDIATE_USE and PRIVILEGE remain excluded, COLLECTIVE_GOAL is excluded as its own category (bundling and bulk discounts describe an individual purchase and have no meaning against a shared pot), and INSURANCE is noted as not sold through the store at all — enrollment is purchased through the insurance interface under FEAT-CLASS-003.

- **The branch rename is executed, and the v1 line now lives under one ref (2026-09-07)** — `main` is the default and only protected branch, `origin/HEAD` resolves to it, and `CTH_v2.0` is gone. Two things differ from the plan and are worth stating rather than discovering later. The v1 `main` became `main_legacy_v1.10.0` instead of `legacy_main`, and the separate `legacy_v1.10.0` branch was deleted; that is safe because the deleted tip (`1f7bfeb40`) is an ancestor of the surviving branch, verified rather than assumed, so **`main_legacy_v1.10.0` is the single ref carrying the v1 line** and every `v1.*` tag remains. `DEVELOPMENT.md` and `.claude/CLAUDE.md` are retargeted accordingly — they named a ref that no longer resolves, which is precisely the defect the rename existed to remove.

  GitHub refused to delete `CTH_v2.0` until the default moved, since a repository cannot delete its own default branch. That makes the ordering a constraint rather than a preference: repoint `origin/HEAD` and move protection first, delete second.

  Deleting the Dependabot branches closed their eight PRs (#1354, #1353, #1287, #1286, #1284, #1281, #1270, #1236). No vulnerability fix was lost — those were `pip` and `github-actions` bumps, a different ecosystem from every open advisory — but Dependabot does not recreate a PR for a version whose PR was closed, so those specific bumps will not return unprompted.

### Removed

- **`codex/ledger-canonicalization` deleted; its "owner decision" was already answered by events (2026-09-07)** — The branch was held open for a decision about replacing `deploy.yml` with an exact-SHA release workflow. Every artifact that decision concerned is already on `main`, having arrived via `599dc7e7b` rather than via this branch. What remained was the branch, not the change. Measured by content instead of commit count, the 22 "unique" commits are net-negative in every file they touch — `status/projection.py` −57, `app/observability.py` −59 — and `status_service/app.py` is the clearest case: it defines `derive_capability_cards` inline with no `derive_platform_checks`, where `main` has moved the first into `status/projection.py` and added the second. It is the earlier draft of the code it would overwrite. Merging it would also have restored `docs/LOGS/`, deleted `constitutional-ci.yml` and `deploy-status.yml`, and dropped roughly twenty test modules. Tagged `archive/ledger-canonicalization-20260907` before deletion, because it was the one branch with no remote and therefore the one deletion that would otherwise have been irreversible.

  The method cost is recorded too: the three-dot diffstat read as though the branch deleted the deploy workflow, when `main` had already deleted it. Three-dot measures from the merge base, so on a stale branch it describes a tree that no longer exists. Two-dot returned 416 files of near-total reversion. Use two-dot to decide disposition; three-dot only to review.

### Added

- **A lapsed collective goal now expires its buy-ins and refunds them (2026-09-07)** — DOM-STORE-001 §5 requires a collective-goal entitlement to record `EXPIRED` "when the goal is not reached by the deadline and coordinate a lawful refund." Only the purchase-time half existed: past the deadline the item stopped selling, but everyone already inside the goal kept a `GRANTED` entitlement they could never exercise and stayed charged for it. `app/feats/collective_goal_expiry_feat.py` (FEAT-STOR-002) closes one lapsed goal in one class, and an hourly job sweeps for them.

  Three properties are deliberate. **Only unmet goals are swept** — a goal reached before its deadline stays `GRANTED`, because that reward is real and expiring it would refund the students who actually won. **Money and entitlement move together**, inside one FEAT envelope, so no failure can leave a refunded student still holding the entitlement or an expired student out of pocket. And it **fails closed per purchase**: if the covering ledger transaction cannot be identified, that purchase group is skipped and reported rather than expired unrefunded — leaving an entitlement `GRANTED` is recoverable, taking a student's money and giving nothing back is not.

  The refund is issued per `correlation_id` group, never per unit, because a bundle is n entitlement lifecycles under one charge and refunding per unit would return the price n times. The refund itself is a reversal — SPEC-OPS-001 §8.1 declines to make "refund" a third ledger primitive, and §8.2 permits the word as user-facing language for the reversal underneath.

  Goal progress had two implementations that disagreed — the student store counted every `GRANTED` event while the teacher store also required `acquisition_type == "PURCHASE"`, so a teacher's direct grant moved one bar and not the other. That is cosmetic until a sweep reads it and the count decides who gets refunded. Both routes now read `app/services/store/collective_goals.py`, which counts buy-ins only and fails closed on an unresolvable target, since treating `target <= 0` as met would silently deny every refund on a misconfigured goal.

### Fixed

- **Ledger corrections voided the money instead of reversing it (2026-09-07)** — SPEC-OPS-001 draws one line and states it twice: *"Money is reversed. Grants are voided"* (§II), and INV-OPS-001 — *"Monetary transactions MUST NOT be voided."* `app/services/ledger_correction_service.py` did both. `void_pending_transaction()` set `is_void`, `status` and `voided_at` on an unsettled charge, and `compensate_posted_transaction()` set `is_void` on the original alongside its compensating entry — which §3.2 forbids directly, since a reversal may not represent the original transaction as never having occurred.

  The mislabelling had two consequences worth separating. **An unsettled charge could be corrected into a windfall.** Store purchases post lazily, so a charge is often still `PENDING` when it is corrected; `settle_balances` skips a voided pending row (`ledger_settlement_service.py:174`), so the debit never landed while the compensating credit did, and the student ended up the full price better off. `transaction_void_feat.py` did both at once for a pending purchase and hit this on the admin void path. **And the snapshot could not be rebuilt from history.** `_posted_history_cents` excludes `is_void` rows, so for every compensated transaction the rebuild dropped the original debit and kept the credit, disagreeing with the incrementally-maintained snapshot by the full amount — which INV-LED-006 requires to agree.

  The module now offers one operation, `reverse_transaction()`, which appends the compensating entry and leaves the original standing apart from the link forward to its reversal. That link is also what enforces one-reversal-per-transaction (INV-LED-013, INV-OPS-005), so callers detecting "already refunded" now test `reversal_transaction_id`, not `is_void`. `void_pending_transaction()` is removed rather than deprecated, per INV-OPS-012. Both legs settle, both reads agree, and pending and posted charges take the same path — the branch that used to distinguish them was itself the artifact of voiding money.

- **The store quoted rent-perk items at $0.00 and the server never honoured it (2026-09-07)** — A student holding an unconsumed `PERK` grant saw the item priced "Rent Perk price: $0.00", the quantity control locked to one unit, bulk discounts suppressed, and the hint "Rent perk active: this purchase is free." Nothing on the server implemented any of it: `_calculate_debit` has no waiver term, no purchase path consults `get_active_rent_grant` (it is imported into `app/routes/api.py` and never called), and `target_account` is written but never read. This was the exact shape of defect #31 — quote one price, charge another — and it went unnoticed only because purchases were not charging at all.

  The waiver was not implemented, because there is nothing to implement. Under DOM-STORE-001 §A a `PERK` acquisition is an entitlement the student **already possesses**; it is redeemed from My Items, not re-bought at zero. So the display was corrected to say so: the card and modal show the real price, the perk badge reads "N free uses in My Items", and the quantity and bulk-discount controls behave as they do for any other item. `is_rent_covered` still displays $0.00 — that button is disabled, so the zero is a statement about coverage rather than a quote for a purchase that can be attempted.

- **Store purchases never took the money (2026-09-07)** — `apply_resolved_ledger_plan` posted the overdraft-protection transfer and the NSF fee and then dropped the principal debit on the floor. Its docstring has claimed it "posts the debit / recovery / overdraft-fee transactions" since it was written, which is most of why the gap survived review: the contract was read instead of the body. The effect was that every store purchase granted its entitlements for free, and an underfunded student could still be charged an NSF fee for a debit that never landed — the one case where the ledger did write a row was the penalty.

  The debit is now posted at the same seam, anchored on `class_id` + `seat_id` with the student as both target and actor, under an idempotency key suffixed `:debit` so it cannot collide with the fee's reservation. `IntendedLedgerPlan` gained a required `transaction_type`: Ledger is domain-blind (DOM-LED-001 §II) and cannot infer what kind of act a debit represents, and a default would have silently mistyped every future caller. Store purchases declare `purchase`, which is what `transaction_void_feat` keys on.

  The purchase description changed from `Store purchase: <name>` to `Purchase: <name> (xN)`. That is not cosmetic — `_void_purchase` parses exactly that format to recover the item and quantity, so under the old wording a purchase could not be voided even once it started charging.

- **The three store sale mechanics were configured, displayed, and then ignored at purchase time (2026-09-07)** — Defects #30/#31/#32 shared one shape: each setting was published and stored correctly, shown on the item card, and never consulted when the purchase executed. A bundle granted one unit while promising N. A bulk discount was quoted on the card and never applied to the debit, so the student consented to one price and was charged another. A collective goal's deadline was displayed and never enforced, so students kept paying into a goal that could no longer be reached.

  Bundles now grant `bundle_quantity` independent entitlement lifecycles per purchase and are priced per bundle — not one entitlement with a counter, because DOM-STORE-001 forbids persisting a remaining-units balance. The bulk discount is recomputed on the authoritative side from Policy configuration and applies to the whole order at or above the threshold, rounded half-up to cents. A purchase against an expired collective goal is refused with `COLLECTIVE_GOAL_EXPIRED` before the ledger is touched. `tests/test_store_sale_mechanics.py` asserts each against the two surfaces the student feels — lifecycles created and amount debited — since the existing publication-validation suite only proves such items cannot be *misconfigured*.

  Not fixed here: DOM-STORE-001:322 also requires a sweep that marks a lapsed goal EXPIRED and refunds lawfully. Only the purchase-time gate exists.

- **The migration chain could not build a database from empty, because the baseline is built from today's ORM (2026-09-07)** — `0001_bootstrap` materializes current ORM metadata at revision 0, so deleting or reshaping a model retroactively changes what the *baseline* contained, and later historical migrations still reference the shape they were written against. Consolidating the store's two tables into one triggered both mirror images of this at once. `store_items` left the ORM, so `0009` failed creating a foreign key to a table that every already-migrated database has; and `store_products` / `store_item_visibility` arrived at revision 0 already carrying their final columns, so `0009` and `e13a59b6aa6b` found the tables present, skipped the creates, and then indexed columns that did not exist yet.

  Fixed at two seams. Bootstrap gained a Section C that recreates retired baseline tables in the minimal shape the chain needs, and hands reshaped tables back to the migrations that own their history by dropping them — guarded on emptiness, raising rather than dropping if rows are present, which makes the "this only ever runs pre-migration" claim structural instead of assumed. `0009` gained one `column_exists` guard, matching the precedent already in that file. The underlying smell is not fixed and should not be forgotten: **a schema-affecting model deletion can break the migration chain without touching a migration file**, so the revision graph looks innocent while a fresh database fails.

- **SPEC-STORE-001 §V product-configuration rules were enforced nowhere, and now run at the one seam that writes a product (2026-09-07)** — The rules lived in `StorePolicyConfigParser`, which validated a JSON policy payload. Nothing has produced such a payload since the catalog became a single typed table, so `grep` found exactly one reference to the class: its own definition. The teacher-facing form posted straight through to `publish_product` with only field-level WTForms checks, which meant a bundled immediate-use item, a collective goal with no deadline, a bulk discount of 150%, or a negative price were all publishable.

  The rules now live in `store_service._validate_definition`, called by `publish_product`, and the dead parser is deleted rather than left beside them — two copies of a rule set with only one of them reachable is how they drift apart, and this is the drift already having happened. `InvalidDefinition` subclasses `StoreServiceError`, which the store route already catches, so a rejected definition surfaces to the teacher as a flash rather than a 500.

  One rule is deliberately narrower than the spec text. SPEC-STORE-001 §V.A forbids bundling on every entitlement type; bundling is permitted here on `DELAYED_USE` and `HALL_PASS`, because those are the two types that can hold more than one unexercised unit, and a blanket ban would remove a mechanic the teacher-facing form offers. **This needs owner review** — either the spec is stale on the point or the toggle should not exist.

- **Store product listings were ordered by a random UUID (2026-09-07)** — `build_policy_list_view` sorted on `product_id`, which read as creation order only by accident: it used to be a sequential integer. It is now a lineage UUID, so the teacher's catalog was arriving in arbitrary order. Sorted on `(created_at, name, policy_uuid)` — when the teacher published it, then alphabetically — with the UUID only breaking ties between versions published in the same instant.

- **Rent no longer creates store items; the store declares which of its products rent grants (2026-09-07)** — This supersedes and reverts an entry that stood here earlier the same day, which described making a rent-created store item *partially* editable by locking the six fields Rent Settings wrote. That fix was correct about the symptom and wrong about the cause. The reason a teacher could not rename a rent perk was that two features both claimed to author the same row, and dividing the fields between them ratifies the double ownership rather than ending it — it also leaves the teacher reading a store item whose name is editable nowhere on the store page.

  Ownership is inverted instead. Rent Settings has no itemization block and creates nothing; the store's add/edit form carries a `[This item is rent linked]` toggle with its quantity, and turning it on writes the product's lineage into the class's rent `satisfaction_benefits`. Every field on a rent-linked product is editable, because the store owns all of them. A rent-linked product takes effect on the **next** cycle, so adding or removing one never re-prices an assessment already issued. `TestRentLinkedItemEditing` and `tests/dom/class/test_rent_itemization_store_gate.py` are deleted with the premise they tested — the latter checked that a rent-created item stays unpurchasable while the store feature is off, which the store surface's own scope gate now covers more strongly, since with the store disabled the product cannot be created at all.

- **Editing an insurance policy now retires the version it replaces, and grouped tiers are editable (2026-09-07)** — `configure_insurance_definition` takes `supersedes_policy_uuid` and retires the predecessor inside the same FEAT context. The old behavior left both versions IN_USE, so students saw a product twice and could buy either; for a grouped policy it was worse than cosmetic, because the original still held its `(class, group, rank)` slot and the edit was *rejected outright*. Both are the same defect. The retire runs **before** `_enforce_tier_group_rules`, so the predecessor vacates the rank as its replacement takes it — one fix, and grouped policies now edit exactly like ungrouped ones.

  This is not new design. `DOM-POL-001` §VI.0 forbids self-referential version pointers, the model's own index comment already said "editing (mint new + retire old) is free," and FEAT-CLASS-003 §VIII.2 described the vacate-then-occupy sequence. Only the caller never performed the retire. RETIRED is the correct projection rather than HIDDEN because it means precisely "not selectable for new work, may remain readable while live dependencies drain" — which is what "old policyholders keep their terms to expiry" requires. Nothing about existing coverage changes: entitlements freeze `policy_uuid` at purchase (§VII), so the retired row remains their contract.

  Validation runs before the retire, so a rejected submission never withdraws a live policy, and the whole sequence is one transaction.

- **A hidden insurance policy can be put back on sale (2026-09-07)** — New `admin.reactivate_insurance_policy` route, surfaced on hidden rows in place of **Hide**. A definition row is immutable, so this is not a flip back to IN_USE: it marshals the hidden row's configuration into a new IN_USE `policy_uuid` and supersedes the hidden one. The teacher-visible result is the product back on the shelf on the terms it was hidden with. Previously hiding was a one-way door in practice — editing a hidden policy just produced another hidden policy — which made **Hide** strictly worse than useless for a teacher who wanted a seasonal product.

- **The non-monetary waiting period is enforced at claim time (2026-09-07)** — `waiting_period_days` was configured, validated, persisted, seeded with real preset values (Basic defaults to seven days), and read by nothing. A student could file a claim minutes after buying a policy sold on the promise of a week's wait. `_enforce_non_monetary_submission` now gates submission on it, supplying the `NON_MONETARY` arm that `_submit_insurance_claim_impl` never had.

  Semantics match the existing filing window rather than inventing a second convention: class-local **calendar** days, not N×24h, so a three-day wait bought at 3pm opens at the start of the third class day, not at 3pm on it. The wait is anchored on the entitlement's own `GRANTED` timestamp and read from the frozen policy contract, which gives tier switching the right behavior for free — an upgrade is a new grant, so its wait starts over, while the superseded coverage is untouched. Zero is honored as immediate coverage.

- **Dependabot raises `npm` alerts it is not configured to fix (2026-09-07)** — All eleven open advisories resolve to one manifest, `docs-site/package-lock.json`, while `.github/dependabot.yml` declares only `pip` and `github-actions`, both at `/`. There is no `npm` entry and none for `/docs-site`, so no pull request can ever be opened against the file the alerts are about; the security tab and the update configuration describe disjoint sets. Scoped rather than escalated: nothing builds or deploys `docs-site/`, its `build/` and `node_modules/` are gitignored, and the application's only touch is reading `route-map.json`, a data file — so the advisories are real but carry no production exposure. Recorded as build-tooling hygiene with the configuration gap named, so the count is not rediscovered later and misread as a neglected backlog.

### Added

- **`/health/status` replaces `/health/deep`, and the status service deploys from CI (2026-09-06)** — The deep health endpoint reported internal diagnostics; the replacement returns bounded signals only — a key, a layer, an outcome, an epistemic state, and a diagnostic code — and deliberately carries no table counts, no tenant data, and no raw exception text. The distinction that makes it useful is `UNKNOWN`/`CHECK_NOT_REGISTERED`: a capability with no registered check reports that it was not observed rather than reporting health, so an unmonitored surface cannot read as a healthy one. `tests/dom/operation/test_health.py` pins both halves — the signal keys and the absence of `class_id` in any signal — and asserts `/health/deep` now 404s, so the retired surface cannot come back unnoticed.

  `derive_capability_cards` and `derive_platform_checks` move to `status/projection.py` and are shared by the public page, which gains a platform-health section. The card's subtitle now renders `card.question` instead of a `key` that had been overloaded to carry the question text with its `?` stripped.

  `.github/workflows/deploy-status.yml` tests and deploys both Cloud Run revisions. Two things were corrected against the ported version. Its final verification step curled `/health/status` on the status service, but that path is the *application's* endpoint — the status service exposes `/health` — so `curl --fail` would have 404'd and failed every deploy *after* both revisions had already gone live, which is the worst possible time to learn the check was pointed at the wrong host. And the path filters triggered on `tests/dom/operation/test_health.py` while the test job never ran it; a trigger that gates on a file it does not execute is the same matches-nothing shape this branch keeps finding, so the filter was narrowed to what the job actually tests. The Postgres service image also moves to the ECR mirror, matching the three other workflows that use one.

- **`github-pages/index.html` is now the v2.0 deployment holding page (2026-09-06)** — The static site can be deployed without inviting anyone into the application. `index.html` previously redirected to `landing.html`, which carries the three sign-in buttons; it now carries "launching soon" content of its own, and `landing.html` is deliberately orphaned from the site root. What that does and does not do is worth stating precisely: `landing.html` is still deployed and still answers on a direct URL, so this removes the *invitation* to sign in, not the access. Whether the application is reachable is decided by the application — maintenance mode and the login routes — and must be verified there rather than inferred from this page.

  Because the page now carries real content it enters `PUBLIC_ROUTES` in `tests/test_axe_compliance.py`. It was never listed before, correctly: a three-line redirect stub has nothing to audit. The audit found a WCAG 2.1 AA violation on its first run — the support email rendered as `--secondary` gold on the `--steward-blue` card at 3.91:1 against a 4.5:1 requirement — now a `mailto:` link in near-white, underlined so it is also distinguishable by more than color (WCAG 1.4.1). A tab-switching script that came along with the content was removed: its `.tab-btn` / `.tab-content` selectors exist only in `learnmore.html`, so on this page it bound zero listeners and was the sixth instance on this branch of a selector that matches nothing while looking satisfied.

- **Documentation links are checked in CI, in three passes that answer three different questions (2026-09-06)** — `.github/workflows/docs-links.yml` runs `lychee --offline` on every PR (does the link resolve in the repository), `md-dead-link-check` on push and schedule (does the external URL still exist), and `scripts/check_production_docs.py` against the live site (does the page actually *render*). The third is not redundant with the first two: the `/docs/` route is audience-gated and template-driven, so a document can be present as a file, link correctly, and still 404 or 500 when served. It is bounded deliberately — same-host only, under `/docs/`, capped at 300 URLs, 2s between requests — because it is the one check in the suite that generates load on production.

  Two defects were fixed in the ported script before it was wired up, both of the failure class this branch keeps rediscovering. The workflow passes `--crawl` and `--delay`, which argparse did not define, so every scheduled run would have exited 2 on "unrecognized arguments" — a gate that fails for a reason unrelated to what it checks tells you nothing about production, and a red build nobody can act on is a build people stop reading. And a crawl that reached the index, found no onward links, and stopped reported success, which is indistinguishable from a docs surface that renders correctly; `--min-urls` (the workflow sets 10) turns "found nothing" into a failure. The `md-dead-link-check` config inherited an `exclude_files` entry for `docs/LOGS/AUDITS/*`, a tree removed on 2026-09-05 that must not be reintroduced, so the exclusion could only ever match nothing; `docs/archive/*` is excluded instead, on the grounds that archived material is superseded by definition and its outbound links record what was once true rather than assert what still holds.

- **`SECRET_KEY` can be rotated without logging everyone out mid-class (2026-09-06)** — `SECRET_KEY_FALLBACKS` is now populated from the environment (comma-separated, oldest last), so a new key can sign while retired keys still verify. The three-step procedure is written into `app/__init__.py` next to the config. Step 2 — how long to wait before retiring the old key — names 60 minutes, the sysadmin idle limit from `app/auth.py` and the longest of the three role lifetimes, and says explicitly *not* to read `PERMANENT_SESSION_LIFETIME` for this: that config is unset, so it is Flask's 31-day default, and it does not bound authenticated cookies. An earlier draft of this comment asserted a two-hour `PERMANENT_SESSION_LIFETIME`, which would have made step 2 wrong in both directions — naming a duration the application does not use, and pointing the operator at a knob that does not govern the thing being waited on. The figure it now cites is enforced on the cookie itself by the change below, so the procedure and the mechanism cannot drift apart silently. `CSRF_SECRET_KEY` is decoupled from `SECRET_KEY` in the same block, because Flask-WTF signs CSRF tokens with a single key and has no fallback mechanism, so a bare `SECRET_KEY` rotation would still break every form rendered before the cutover. It is set conditionally: Flask-WTF reads it via `config.get(name, secret_key)`, so a present-but-`None` entry shadows the `SECRET_KEY` default and raises "CSRF is not configured."
- **Constitutional CI: changed paths select invariant families, and the selected evidence is actually executed (2026-09-05)** — `.ci/invariant_families.yml` declares eight families (`CI-ARC-EXEC`, `CI-SCOPE`, `CI-TEMPORAL`, `CI-PERSIST`, `CI-PII`, `CI-XDOMAIN`, `CI-RENDER`, `CI-VALIDATION`), each naming the invariants it governs, the paths that select it, the evidence that discharges it, and — where no sufficient evidence exists — saying so in `known_limits` rather than declaring a pass. `scripts/ci_classifier.py` performs selection only and can never emit a PASS; `scripts/ci_evidence_runner.py` runs the evidence and aggregates fail-closed, `FAIL > BLOCKED > NOT_EVALUATED > PASS`. A family with no declared evidence reports `NOT_EVALUATED`, which is not a pass and fails the build. This is deliberately **advisory** — not a required status check — until it has run green on a real PR. At the time it landed, `CI-XDOMAIN` genuinely had no evidence and selected on nearly every application path, so most PRs reported `NOT_EVALUATED`; reporting that honestly is the feature, and that gap is closed in the entry above. `CI-PII` remains evidence-less and still selects `app/models.py` and `migrations/**`, so migration and model changes continue to aggregate to `NOT_EVALUATED` — that is the last gap before promotion.
- **`CI-XDOMAIN` now discharges INV-ARC-021 with real evidence instead of reporting `NOT_EVALUATED` on nearly every PR (2026-09-05)** — The family governed INV-ARC-021 while declaring no evidence, and selected on `app/feats/**`, `app/routes/**`, `app/services/**`, and `migrations/**`. Because the runner aggregates fail-closed, that made most substantive PRs exit 1 for a cause unrelated to the change under review — honest, but a gate that is always red is a gate people learn to ignore, and it is what kept the workflow out of required status checks. Closed by taking both directions the invariant actually licenses, not the one that was convenient. **Evidence:** `scripts/validate-cross-domain.py` answers the two questions INV-ARC-021 makes statically decidable. §V.2/§V.6 — the FEAT layer is the sole coordination layer, so no service or FEAT may import a route, and no domain service may import a coordination FEAT; `app.feats.base` is excepted because it is the execution harness (FEATContext, correlation ids) a service legitimately enlists in, not a coordination unit, and treating it as one would have produced nine false positives. §V.7 — "the only legal cross-domain foreign key targets are shared anchor columns: `class_id`, `seat_id`, and `user_id`" — with table ownership read from DOM-CORE-002 §V under its §IV.2 rule that each table has exactly one owning domain, the only canon that makes "cross-domain" objectively decidable at the schema level. Both are AST checks over `app/**` and `app/models.py`: no database, no application import. Of 89 declared foreign keys, 65 target the three anchors and 24 are judged. **Selection:** `app/routes/**` was dropped and `app/models.py` added. There is no `app/domains/**` tree and no normative module-to-domain map — MAP-CORE-001 is marked *Informative* and its FEAT names do not match real module names — so nothing about a routes-only change can be falsified without inventing authority, and selecting it anyway would have rebuilt the `CI-RENDER` vacuous-pass defect one entry down in this list. INV-ARC-021 is not thereby left ungoverned for routes: `CI-ARC-EXEC` already names it, already selects `app/routes/**`, and runs FEAT-enforcement evidence plus `policy_guardrails`; a test asserts an INV-ARC-021 family is still selected for a route change so the narrowing cannot quietly become a coverage hole. **Baseline:** the check runs against 11 enumerated pre-existing violations — `ledger_fee_service` and `payroll/settlement` importing coordination FEATs, 6 cross-domain foreign keys, and 3 findings on tables absent from DOM-CORE-002 §V, which are reported as unattributed rather than assigned an owner this change has no authority to assign. Remediating them needs migrations and module relocation and is out of scope. The baseline is not a mute button: a **new** violation fails the build, and so does a baseline entry that **no longer reproduces**, because an exemption for a defect that has since been fixed is precisely the matches-nothing failure class this system exists to catch — the sixth instance on this branch would otherwise have been one we wrote ourselves. `tests/test_cross_domain_validator.py` pins both directions (a service importing a coordination FEAT errors, importing `app.feats.base` does not; a cross-domain FK errors, an anchor-targeted, self-referential, or intra-domain FK does not; relative and plain-`import` forms both resolve; composite `ForeignKeyConstraint` declarations are extracted), plus the baseline's own decay in both directions. A change under `app/services/**` now aggregates to **PASS** on earned evidence. `CI-PII` is now the only evidence-less family remaining.
- **The evidence manifest now self-checks before it is trusted (2026-09-05)** — `ci_evidence_runner.py --self-check` runs as the workflow's first step and fails the build if any `path_rules` glob matches zero tracked files at HEAD, any `evidence_commands` entry names a file that does not exist, or any `auxiliary_evidence` id has no backing script. The reasoning is the failure class this repository keeps rediscovering: **a rule that matches nothing produces exactly the same output as a rule that was satisfied.** Selection is driven entirely by path rules, so a stale glob silently converts a mandatory gate into no gate at all, and nothing in the result JSON distinguishes the two. It found a real instance immediately — see below.

### Changed

- **The landing pages are not deployed before launch; they wait on a branch (2026-09-07)** — `github-pages/landing.html` and `learnmore.html` are removed from this branch and live on `launch/v2-landing-pages`. Merging that branch at launch is the act of publishing them, and on that branch the landing page occupies `index.html` rather than sitting beside it — the holding page is only true until launch, which is exactly when the merge happens, so the two never coexist and never disagree about whether the product has shipped. The `landing.html` filename does not survive the merge: a second name serving the same content would be a second front door to keep in sync, and nothing ever linked to it, since the file was never deployed. This supersedes the arrangement in the entry below, which kept both files deployed and merely unlinked from `index.html`. That was not enough, and the entry said so at the time: an unlinked page is not an unreachable one, so `landing.html` still answered on a direct URL with its three sign-in buttons intact. The holding page removed the *invitation* to sign in and left the access — a distinction that is fine to document and bad to ship, because it makes the site's safety depend on nobody guessing a filename. Not deploying the file is the only version of the claim that is actually true. The application's own gating (maintenance mode, the login routes) is a separate question and still has to be verified on its own terms; this change just stops the static site from being the thing that undermines it.

  The deletion is a commit on this branch and the launch branch is cut *after* it, re-adding both files. That ordering matters: branching first and deleting second would leave git treating the branch as already merged, so the launch merge would restore nothing. The audit's equality assertion — the list must equal the `.html` files on disk — is what makes the return safe, since the two pages fail the suite until they are listed again.

- **The application no longer serves the marketing site, and `MARKETING_SITE_URL` is now actually configured (2026-09-06)** — `github-pages/` is published to GitHub Pages and answers on its own host; it was *also* being served by the app at `/gh/<path>`, which existed only so a local certification run could stay on one origin. That route is deleted, along with the two rewrites it required — mapping the landing page's absolute sign-in links back to the serving origin, and swapping the CDN font block for the app's own stylesheet because the published markup was subject to the application CSP. Both existed solely to make one file correct under two hosts. With one host they are unnecessary, and `app/routes/main.py` loses a regex, a file read, a `safe_join`, and four imports.

  Removing the route surfaced why it was still load-bearing. `MARKETING_SITE_URL` was read at `main.py` with `.get()` and **assigned nowhere** — no config module, no environment template, no workflow — so `if marketing_url:` had never been true in any environment, and `/` fell through to the app-served copy *in production as well as locally*. The branch that was supposed to send visitors to the marketing host had never executed once. It is now set in `app.config` from the environment, defaulting to the CNAME in `github-pages/CNAME` — the host where Pages actually answers, rather than the `timwonderer.github.io` URL the now-removed `GITHUB_PAGES_URL` defaulted to. `/privacy`, `/terms`, and `/district` were redirecting through that second, separately-defaulted config key naming the same site; both collapse into one.

  The accessibility audit moved rather than shrank, which was the other reason to be careful here. `tests/test_axe_compliance.py` drove a browser against the local Flask server, so `/gh/` was the only reason the marketing pages were auditable at all — deleting the route would have quietly reduced `PUBLIC_ROUTES` to `/`, leaving a green gate that covered nothing. It now serves `github-pages/` over a throwaway `http.server` on an ephemeral port and needs no Flask at all, which also removes the skip-when-no-server-is-listening branch that made an unrun audit look like a pass in aggregate output. A second test asserts the audited list equals the `.html` files on disk, so a page added to the directory without being listed fails the suite instead of shipping unaudited.

- **`CTH_v2.0` becomes `main`; the v1 `main` is retired (2026-09-06)** — Resolves the decision recorded in `PRODUCTION_READINESS_2026-09` §V, where `deploy.yml` shipped production from a `main` that was 1053 commits behind: merge v2 into `main`, or repoint the deploy trigger. Neither — the branch is renamed. `main` → `legacy_main`, `CTH_v2.0` → `main`, then `legacy_main` is deleted; the v1 line survives on `legacy_v1.10.0`, so nothing is lost. The point is not cosmetic. Every workflow filter, SOP, contribution instruction, and migration procedure in this repository already names `main` by default, and the branch that carries the code has been called something else for the entire v2 effort. That mismatch is what produced five separate inert CI gates on this branch, each filtering on a ref that did not exist and each therefore reporting nothing while appearing to pass. Renaming the branch to the name everything already assumes removes the source of that defect class rather than relocating it to a third name.

  In-repo references were triaged three ways and only the first was rewritten. **Binding** — a filter or instruction that must resolve — was retargeted: four workflows, `SOP-DB-009`/`-010`/`-011`, `SOP-DEP-022`/`-023`, `SOP-DEV-002a`, `README.md`, `DEVELOPMENT.md`, and `CLAUDE.md`. **Descriptive** — prose in `INV-ARC-017`, `INV-ARC-020`, `SOP-TEST-003`, and `MAP-ADV-001` that identified the *codebase* by branch name — had the name dropped in favor of "v2", because a branch was never the right identifier for a codebase and substituting a new branch name would preserve the error. **Dated record** — `CHANGELOG.md`, dated audits, everything under `docs/archive/`, and the tracker's own narrative — was left intact, because rewriting it would falsify what was true at the time.

  One consequence is easy to miss and is now written down. `DEVELOPMENT.md` carried two items reading "port launch-critical `origin/main` deltas," where `origin/main` meant the v1 branch. After the rename that instruction reads as porting `main` into itself, so both now measure against `legacy_v1.10.0` explicitly. Instructions that say "never merge to `main`" likewise describe the former v1 branch and no longer apply.

- **Credential hashing is centralized behind one primitive, with the KDF profile pinned rather than inherited (2026-09-06)** — `hash_password()` / `verify_password()` in `app/hash_utils.py` are now the only path to the password KDF; no direct `generate_password_hash` or `check_password_hash` call remains anywhere in `app/` or `tests/`. This is SPEC-SEC-001 §V.1.1 and §V.1.5, binding through the INV-ARC-019 incorporation in the entry below. `verify_password` deliberately takes `(plaintext, stored_hash)` — the reverse of Werkzeug's order — so every migrated call site swaps its arguments; a site that did not would fail closed and therefore never announce itself, which is why the order is now pinned by a test.

  The profile is written out as `PASSWORD_KDF = "scrypt:32768:8:1"` and passed explicitly, even though Werkzeug 3.1.8's default already equals it. Relying on the default would be a coincidence a dependency upgrade can end, and the resulting drift is invisible in both directions: new hashes would simply be minted under different parameters, while every existing hash kept verifying, because the encoded verifier carries its own parameters. Nothing in the application would observe the change. `tests/test_credential_primitive.py` asserts the constant against the literal from the spec (comparing it to itself would pass under any value) and reads the profile back out of a produced verifier rather than trusting the constant; a third test skips today with a note explaining that it becomes load-bearing on precisely the upgrade the pin exists to survive.

- **A malformed stored verifier crashed the login handler instead of failing closed (2026-09-06)** — Found by the new §V.1.6 test, not by reading. Werkzeug is only partly fail-closed: `check_password_hash` catches the `ValueError` from splitting a verifier with too few `$`-separated fields, but one that splits into three fields with an empty or unrecognized method reaches `_hash_internal` and raises `ValueError: Invalid hash method ''`. That exception would have escaped into the login route as a 500, which distinguishes "this account's stored verifier is damaged" from "wrong passphrase" by response code alone — exactly the disclosure §V.1.6 prohibits. A damaged verifier is also what a partially completed credential migration leaves behind, so this is a reachable state rather than a theoretical one. `verify_password` now returns False for every unusable verifier without distinguishing which condition occurred, and the test parametrizes six shapes including a well-formed hash under a foreign algorithm.

  Also verified by the same file: `PEPPER_KEY`, `SECRET_KEY`, and `ENCRYPTION_KEY` are mutated between hashing and verifying and the credential still verifies, proving none of them reaches the KDF (§V.1.3). If any did, the failure would surface as every credential in the system going invalid at once, during a routine key rotation.

- **`SPEC-SEC-001` is now incorporated by the three invariants that own its subject matter, and is tracked in the repository (2026-09-06)** — The specification says of itself (§III) that it "is not an independent level of runtime authority" and is "binding only where incorporated by a governing `INV-*`, `DOM-*`, or `FEAT-*` contract." Nothing incorporated it, and the file was untracked, so every requirement it stated — including the `scrypt:32768:8:1` profile and the §V.1a startup gate — bound nothing at all. It is now committed alongside `SOP-SEC-001`, and `SOP-DOC-000` classifies `docs/SPEC/` as Tier 2 while stating explicitly that `SPEC` is a technical-contract namespace, not a runtime authority tier, so the classification cannot be misread as promoting it above `DOM-*`.

  Incorporation was written into three documents chosen by **ownership of the subject**, not by the spec's own §IV dependency list — that list is the specification pointing *up* at what governs it, and reading it as a to-do list would have wired eleven invariants, most of which have nothing to say about credentials. `INV-ARC-019` §VI is the primary: it assigns `users` ownership of "authentication credentials" but never says how one is constructed, and no `DOM-*` sits above credential material (`DOM-IDEN-002`/`-003` name `pin_hash` and `passphrase_hash` and deliberately specify no algorithm), so §VI is the only incorporating authority available. It takes §V.1 (canonical KDF, fixed profile, no application secret as input, verifier-only storage, fail-closed verification), §V.2 for lookup digests, §V.3.1, and §V.4. `INV-ARC-018` §V owns the two permitted storage *forms* and takes §V.2's construction rules plus §V.5 as the code-level expression of its own §VIII retention rule. `INV-ARC-016` §VII already required `AUDIT_HMAC_KEY` at startup independently and takes only that variable's row from the §V.1a table plus the rotation-independence paragraph.

  Two things are stated in the incorporating text so they are not rediscovered as preferences. **`PEPPER_KEY` is not a credential input** — it keys deterministic lookup digests only; peppering passwords would couple every credential to a key whose rotation is already a lookup-digest migration event, silently converting it into a credential-invalidating one, which §V.1.3 prohibits and §V.1a's independent-rotation rule forbids in general. And the KDF profile is fixed **by specification rather than by a library default**, so code must not rely on a dependency to supply it; a default that happens to match is a coincidence a future upgrade may end.

  One gap is recorded rather than papered over. §V.1a requires refusal to start when a required variable is "absent, blank, malformed, or uses a development/default value"; `app/__init__.py` checks only absent and blank. `INV-ARC-016` §VII therefore incorporates only the absent/blank gate it has always stated and says in the document that the malformed and development-default conditions are a known gap needing their own change and their own verification — because incorporating the broader clause would have created a live constitutional violation on landing, out of a documentation commit, with nothing failing to announce it.

- **Ledger decomposed into per-concern services (2026-09-05)** — `app/services/ledger_service.py` and `app/services/balance_service.py` are replaced by dedicated command, correction, posting, settlement, transfer, balance-query, and interest services; `app/utils/banking.py` becomes a thin re-export of the settlement service. Absorbed from `codex/ledger-canonicalization`. The substantive gain is in settlement: it now allocates `posting_sequence` under a class-row lock, which is what makes the verification path (`reconstruct_posted_balance`, `verify_posted_balance`) answerable at all — without a posting boundary those reads returned `missing_posting_boundary` indefinitely, so the ledger's proof machinery was present but inert. Two guarantees from the previous implementation were carried forward into the new settlement service rather than lost in the swap: account-type normalization is enum-aware (a bare `str().lower()` on an `AccountType` member yields `"accounttype.checking"`, which would have minted a third snapshot scope and stranded that money outside both real accounts), and a lazily-created snapshot row is seeded from posted history rather than from zero (seeding at zero is only correct if a missing snapshot implies no posted history; it does not, and inserting a zero row suppresses the aggregate fallback that had been answering correctly, erasing the seat's balance from every read). The incoming `default="checking"` on `LedgerBalanceSnapshot.account_type` was rejected: a default does not make an unnamed account safe, it makes it silently checking, which is the bug class the per-account snapshot split existed to fix.

### Fixed

- **A missing telemetry package took down the whole application, not just telemetry (2026-09-06)** — `app/observability.py` imported `prometheus_client` at module scope, and `create_app()` imports that module, so an incomplete install failed application startup rather than the observability surface. SPEC-OPS-003 §VII is explicit that telemetry failure "MUST NOT change the result, transaction boundary, authorization, rollback behavior, or user-visible business outcome"; a module-scope import is the harshest available violation of it. The symptom was also maximally unhelpful — it surfaced as a pytest collection error with no visible connection to metrics. The import is now guarded behind `TELEMETRY_AVAILABLE`, with `record_request()` returning early and `metrics_payload()` returning `b""` when the package is absent. The package remains pinned in `requirements.txt` and is expected to be present; the guard governs only what happens when it is not.

  Recording is separately wrapped, because `record_request` runs in an `after_request` hook where an exception would reach the client as a failed request — the same §VII prohibition, reached by a different route. `tests/test_observability_degradation.py` proves the degraded path by reloading the module with the import refused, which required unregistering the collectors first: `prometheus_client` metric objects self-register into a process-global registry at construction, so reloading the module a second time raises `Duplicated timeseries` and the test would have failed for a reason having nothing to do with the behavior under test. The third test asserts `"admin.dashboard" not in CAPABILITY_BY_ENDPOINT` *before* asserting the unmapped endpoint is dropped, so it cannot pass vacuously if the capability map is later extended.

- **`SPEC-OPS-003` used a header no other namespaced document uses, and omitted three required sections (2026-09-06)** — It was the only namespaced document in the repository carrying a `| Field | Value |` header instead of the `| Reference Number | Version | Effective Date | Supersedes | Authority Level |` table `SOP-DOC-000` requires, and it had no **Scope**, **Authority Level**, or **Dependencies** sections at all. Raised to v1.1 with the required sections added and later sections renumbered to preserve §VIII's mandated order; the old header's `Authority` content is preserved in the new §III rather than dropped. Verified safe to renumber by grepping for inbound citations first — only a file-path link in `SOP-DOC-002` and a note in `SPEC-OPS-004` exist, and no section-level citation of this document exists anywhere, so nothing orphans. No normative rule was added, removed, or altered.

- **Documentation index was 44% dead links, and live specs cited archived material as governing authority (2026-09-06)** — Two failure modes, both invisible by construction. A markdown link that resolves to nothing renders as ordinary text on GitHub and a 404 in the docs site; neither fails a build, so `SOP-DOC-002_DOCUMENTATION_INDEX` — the map of the documentation — had accumulated 69 dead links out of 158 without anything reporting it. Its §VI stated that ~90 v1 documents "remain active for reference," while two-thirds of those paths pointed at repository-root directories (`ARCHITECTURE/`, `FEATURES/`, `SECURITY/`) deleted in the v2 reorganization. Separately, four live documents — `INV-ARC-017` (Constitutional), `MAP-ADV-001`, `SOP-TEST-001`, and `SOP-TEST-003` — declared `docs/archive/v2-tracking-2026/V2_Full_compliance_migration_plan.md` in their **Dependencies** sections, and two of them additionally cited `docs/TRACKING/V2_REBUILD_VALIDATION_REPORT.md`, a path that had itself been moved into the archive without the citation following. Archived material is superseded by definition, so it cannot govern anything; an unlabelled link to it is indistinguishable from a citation of current authority, which is the exact confusion the archive exists to prevent.

  All 87 surviving index links now resolve. §VI and §VII were replaced with archive pointers and a supersession table rather than repaired link-by-link — enumerating superseded documents under a heading claiming they are active reproduced both failures at once. `DEVELOPMENT.md`'s Quick Links were rebuilt against live documents; nine of its ten entries were dead or pointed into the archive unlabelled, including four archived trackers presented as the active ones when the tracker of record is `docs/TRACKING/PRODUCTION_READINESS_2026-09.md`. `MAP-UI-001`'s audit inputs are legitimate historical provenance and were kept, but relabelled and repointed at their real archive paths. `SOP-DOC-000` §V gained the governing rule.

  `tests/test_docs_archive_citations.py` enforces all three properties so the rot cannot return silently. Its first draft could not fail: the label check searched each line for the word "archive," but every archive link contains that substring in its own path, so every citation vouched for itself. That is the same matches-nothing defect the module exists to catch — a guard that matches nothing is indistinguishable from one that is satisfied — and it was found only by deliberately injecting violations and confirming each assertion fires. The corrected check excises the path before looking for the label, and governs citations of archived *documents* rather than mentions of the archive directory, which are self-evidently not claims of authority.

- **Payroll paid the hardcoded default rate whenever a display label failed to match, at 8× under the configured rate (2026-09-06)** — Tracked as **B11**. The readers resolved `PayrollSettings` on `(class_id, block)`, matching `block` against `ClassEconomy.section`, which INV-ARC-014 §V forbids outright: execution must not depend on labels, sections, or periods. The tracker had carried this as non-blocking with the question "promote to blocking if it can select the wrong rate." It can — but by the opposite mechanism from the one that question anticipates, and the real defect is worse for being quiet.

  It was never a cross-tenant leak; `class_id` was on every query, and every read site derived `section` from the same `class_id` it queried with, so the two could not disagree. The failure runs the other way. `uq_payroll_settings_active_scope` is UNIQUE on `class_id` alone `WHERE availability_state = 'IN_USE'`, so a class has **exactly one** selectable policy row. The reader asked for `block = <section>` and on a miss fell back to `block IS NULL`; whenever that single row's `block` was a non-null string not equal to the class's section, **both queries missed the only row that existed** and every seat in the class was paid `DEFAULT_PAY_RATE_PER_SECOND`. No error, no log — just wrong money. Measured against a fixture configured at 2.00/minute, an 8× silent underpayment. Three routes into that state, none exotic: `ClassEconomy.section` is mutable, so configuring payroll and then renaming the section is sufficient; `block` sits in `PayrollSettings._FROZEN_POLICY_FIELDS`, so it is submittable and carried forward from the predecessor on every subsequent submission, propagating a stale value untouched and never revalidating it; and rows predating the class_id-only scope. The writer was already canonical — `upsert_payroll_settings` scopes by `class_id` alone, and the model's own `__table_args__` comment states that `block` "is display metadata and is never a scoping key" — so writer and reader disagreed about what identifies a policy, and only the reader was wrong.

  Two further instances of the same defect class were found by sweeping for it rather than fixing only what was reported. `insurance_claim_feat._resolve_hourly_pay_rate` filtered `block IS NULL`, so a class whose policy row carried *any* label adjudicated PRODUCTIVITY claims at the fallback rate — a claim and a payroll run priced the same work differently. And `scheduled_tasks.py` resolved the daily limit only `if class_row.section`, so a class with no section label never had its configured limit enforced by auto tap-out at all; that one fails permissive, which is why nothing had reported it.

  `_fetch_active_setting(*, class_id)` is now the single canonical reader and `class_id` is the whole scope. `get_pay_rate_for_block(block, *, class_id)` is replaced by `get_pay_rate_for_class(*, class_id)`, and `get_daily_limit_seconds` loses its `block` parameter. Because the partial unique index guarantees one row, a second row means the index is not holding, so the reader **raises** rather than tie-breaking on newest — picking a winner there would let a schema failure present itself as a pay rate. `_get_batch_pay_rates` is keyed by `class_id` with deterministic ordering so the batch reader that `calculate_payroll_breakdown` prices from cannot disagree with the scalar reader a student is shown; that agreement, not either value alone, is what `tests/dom/prod/test_payroll_rate_scope_is_class_id.py` pins. `app/feats/prod.py` loses a duplicated hardcoded fallback and delegates to the shared reader, keeping its class-existence guard while dropping the dead label binding. The regression file is 6 tests, verified non-vacuous by replicating the pre-fix query verbatim against the same fixture — old reader `0.004166…`, new reader `0.033333…` — because a test that passes for the wrong reason is this same failure class one level up.

- **The audit signing key was enforced everywhere except where the invariant says it must be (2026-09-06)** — INV-ARC-016 §VII requires `AUDIT_HMAC_KEY` to be "required at application startup. The application shall refuse to start if this variable is absent." It was not in `required_env_vars` in `app/__init__.py`, so it wasn't checked at startup at all. `audit_service` does raise on a missing key, but it wraps its own `_load_signing_key()` in `try/except RuntimeError` so the module can import under pytest before fixtures run, leaving `_SIGNING_KEY = b""`. The net behavior — confirmed by booting the app with the key emptied, not by reading the code — was that a deployment missing the key started clean, passed every health check, and then raised `AuditContextError` at the *first audited write*: mid-transaction, inside a FEAT, in front of a user. The system failed closed, which is the correct direction, but at the worst possible moment and on a box that had looked healthy since deploy. Deferred enforcement is not enforcement; "refuse to start" can only be honored at start. `AUDIT_HMAC_KEY` is now in `required_env_vars`.

  Two pieces of documentation were actively misleading here and are corrected. `audit_protected()` in `app/feats/base.py` documented itself as doing "nothing if `AUDIT_HMAC_KEY` is absent (dev/test without the key configured — CI enforces the key in production)". Every clause was false: it re-raises `AuditContextError` rather than doing nothing, no CI job references `AUDIT_HMAC_KEY` anywhere (`grep` across `.github/` and `.ci/` returns nothing), and a fail-open audit hook would itself violate INV-ARC-016. That docstring was the more dangerous half of the defect — someone auditing for a fail-open gap would have found one *described* there and stopped, while the actual gap sat upstream in the startup gate. Separately, the README's `.env` setup block omitted the key entirely, so following the quickstart now produced a hard boot failure; it also used a **quoted** `<< 'EOF'` heredoc, which suppresses `$(...)` expansion and wrote the literal text `$(python3 -c "import secrets; ...")` into `.env` as the value of every secret. Both are fixed. `tests/test_startup_env_requirements.py` parametrizes over the whole required list — so removing *any* name from the gate fails a test — and includes a negative control, because a gate that rejected every environment would otherwise pass.

- **The session cookie outlived every session it represented (2026-09-06)** — three authoritative session lifetimes already existed in `app/auth.py`: a student's hard cap from login (`User.current_session_expires_at`), a teacher's `SESSION_TIMEOUT_MINUTES` idle limit, and a sysadmin's `SYSTEM_ADMIN_SESSION_TIMEOUT_MINUTES` idle limit. All three are enforced server-side, which means they only fire when a request arrives. Nothing bounded the *cookie*: `PERMANENT_SESSION_LIFETIME` is unset, so it was Flask's 31-day default, and a student who closed the tab left a cookie on the device for a month — carrying the `display_metadata` cache, which holds decrypted first and last names for the student and their teacher. `app/session_lifetime.py` adds `RoleScopedSessionInterface`, which derives the cookie's expiry per role in `get_expiration_time`; the student login flow stamps the authoritative instant into the session so the cookie and the `users` row die together rather than approximately together. No new lifetime is introduced and no existing one is changed — the cookie is made to mirror rules that were already binding. A single global `PERMANENT_SESSION_LIFETIME` could not have expressed this: setting it to the ten-minute student cap would have silently cut sysadmin sessions from 60 minutes to 10, which `test_sysadmin_is_not_truncated_to_the_student_limit` now pins against.

  **`PERMANENT_SESSION_LIFETIME` was deliberately left alone, and this is the record of why.** A session with no recognized role falls through to the Flask default on purpose. A pre-login session holds a CSRF token and flash messages; the two session keys that carry personal data, `display_metadata` and `teacher_display_name_cache`, are both written only after a canonical authenticated context exists. The generic permanent-session default is therefore *not* a PII-retention control, and shortening it would trade a working login form — whose CSRF token would start outliving its own cookie — for protection of data that window never holds. This is written down so that rediscovering the 31-day default six months from now does not lead to "hardening" it for a reason that was already investigated and rejected. `test_permanent_session_lifetime_is_left_at_the_flask_default` pins the non-change; `app/session_lifetime.py`'s module docstring carries the same scope note next to the code.
- **The constitutional evidence runner had never executed a single test (2026-09-05)** — `_command_for` built `[pytest_executable, "-q", path]`, and the workflow passes `--pytest python`, so every piece of evidence ran as `python -q tests/dom/.../test_foo.py` — a bare script invocation, which collects nothing. The gate designed to prove invariants held was running Python files as programs and reading their exit codes. It happened to fail closed rather than pass falsely: all ten evidence modules raise on import when run outside pytest, so each returned `1` and each family reported `FAIL`. That is the safe direction, but it means Constitutional CI would have gone red on every PR for a reason having nothing to do with the change under review, and the natural response to a gate that is always red is to stop believing it. Now `[pytest_executable, "-m", "pytest", "-q", path]`. Verified end to end: `CI-TEMPORAL` reports `26 passed`, where it previously returned exit 1 with an import traceback.
- **Two invariant-family path rules selected on directories that have never existed (2026-09-05)** — `CI-XDOMAIN` listed `app/domains/**` and `app/jobs/**`. `git log` confirms neither path has appeared in this repository's history. Because selection is a union across rules, a dead rule does not error — it simply contributes nothing, and the family quietly narrows. Found by the new manifest self-check on its first run, which is the point of building it: this is the fifth occurrence of the matches-nothing class on this branch, and the first one caught by a machine instead of by reading.
- **The migration idempotency gate rejected the guard-clause form, blocking every migration PR (2026-09-05)** — `check_idempotency` in `scripts/validate-migrations.py` recognized a protected operation only when it was lexically nested inside an `if`. Two committed migrations (`a1c4e7d92f30`, `e6f7a8b9c0d1`) use the equivalent early-return form — `if column_exists(...): return` followed by unindented `op.add_column(...)` — and were reported as unguarded. Neither is a real defect; the protection is identical. But the false positive made the validator exit 1, and the validator is `CI-PERSIST`'s auxiliary evidence, so every migration-touching PR would have failed the constitutional gate for a cause no author could act on. A guard clause now counts as a guard: after an `if` whose body ends in `return` or `raise`, the following siblings in that body are conditional. `tests/test_migration_validator.py` pins the check in both directions — a genuinely unguarded `add_column` still errors, an `if` that falls through still protects nothing, and a guard clause does not leak into a later function — because a validator that has been loosened without a test for the loosening is the next silent gate.
- **`CI-VALIDATION` governed the CI machinery but declared no evidence (2026-09-05)** — the family owns `.github/workflows/**`, `scripts/**`, and `tests/conftest.py` under INV-ARC-017, and its `pass_contract` reads "classifier and evidence metadata validate successfully" — but `evidence_commands` was empty, so it could only ever report `NOT_EVALUATED`. The evidence existed the whole time; it was simply never named. `tests/test_ci_classifier.py`, `tests/test_ci_evidence_runner.py`, and `tests/test_migration_validator.py` are now declared, and a CI-only change reports a real PASS.
- **The classifier ate the leading dot of every dotfile path (2026-09-05)** — `changed_paths`, `select_families`, and `classify` each normalized with `path.strip().lstrip("./")`. `lstrip` removes a character *set*, not a prefix, so `.github/workflows/x` arrived as `github/workflows/x` and `.ci/invariant_families.yml` as `ci/invariant_families.yml`. Neither matched any rule. The consequence was disguised rather than absent: with nothing matched, the fail-closed unknown-path fallback fired and selected `CI-ARC-EXEC`, `CI-SCOPE`, and `CI-VALIDATION` — a set that happens to contain the family a workflow change *should* have selected, so the output looked right while the mechanism was broken, and every CI-configuration change silently pulled in seven unrelated database-backed test modules. `CI-VALIDATION` could never be selected by its own `.github/workflows/**` rule. Now normalized with `removeprefix("./")` through a single shared helper, so the three call sites cannot drift apart. Separately, `.ci/**` was governed by no family at all: the manifest that defines every gate was outside the reach of all of them, and is now a `CI-VALIDATION` path rule. Both are pinned by tests that assert selection happens by rule rather than by fallback, since the fallback is precisely what made this invisible.
- **`CI-RENDER` reported PASS while auditing zero templates (2026-09-05)** — `tests/test_accessibility.py` parametrizes over `ACCESSIBILITY_TEMPLATE_PATHS`, and `constitutional-ci.yml` never set it. An unset variable yields an empty parameter set; pytest reports `got empty parameter set for (template_path)`, exits 0, and the family reports PASS. This is the worst form of the matches-nothing class seen on this branch — the other five either failed closed or reported `NOT_EVALUATED`, and the manifest self-check cannot catch this one because the path rule matches, the file exists, and the command succeeds. The workflow now collects changed templates from the same diff the classifier uses, mirroring `accessibility-gate.yml`, with deletions excluded because the audit renders every file it is given. The gate is still not complete and `known_limits` now says how: the audit skips layouts, macros, components, named fragments, and any template containing `{% extends`, which is 52 of 86 top-level templates, so a PR touching only those still passes on nothing.
- **`CI-PII` declared no evidence, and the PII gate it should have declared had never run, was wrong, and could have passed on nothing (2026-09-05)** — Four separate defects stacked in one family. (1) `CI-PII` governs INV-ARC-005/018/019 and selects on `app/models.py`, `app/services/identity/**`, `migrations/**`, and `tests/dom/identity/**` — nearly every schema or identity change — but `evidence_commands` and `auxiliary_evidence` were both empty, so it could only report `NOT_EVALUATED`, and the fail-closed aggregate meant those PRs failed the constitutional gate for a reason unrelated to the change. (2) `scripts/validate-pii-storage.py` already existed and was already registered in `ALLOWED_AUXILIARY` as `pii_storage_validator`, but **no family referenced it**, so it had never executed once. The manifest self-check cannot catch this: it validates that declared ids resolve, not that registered scripts are declared. (3) It would have failed on the live tree if it had run, because it classified `IdentityProfile.notes` as an unamended §VI identity PII field. That reading was wrong. `notes` is teacher-authored free text about a student, and the application neither inspects nor interprets its contents — it is not one of the identity fields §VI enumerates, and the enforceable contract is its storage *form*: arbitrary author text must be encrypted at rest under §V.2 regardless of what is in it. It is now declared in a separate `OPAQUE_ENCRYPTED` category on exactly that basis, while a *new, undeclared* free-text column on a governed identity class is still a finding under §VII.2, which names those columns as leak vessels. (4) The validator selected its classes and columns by name, so renaming `IdentityProfile` — or dropping an allowlisted column — would have left it auditing nothing and printing success, the same matches-nothing class that produced `CI-RENDER`'s vacuous PASS. Absence is now a finding in both directions: a governed class missing from `app/models.py` fails, and an allowlist entry matching no column fails. Beyond the storage half, **§VIII retention had no evidence anywhere in the tree** — nothing asserted that PII is destroyed with the record that owns it, and a correctly encrypted name that survives the deletion of its seat is still retained PII. `tests/dom/identity/test_pii_retention_deletion.py` exercises all four §VIII rules against a real database, including one test that deletes a seat in raw SQL so the schema's `ON DELETE CASCADE` is what is under test rather than the deletion helper — otherwise any other path that deletes a seat would strand its profile. Every test first asserts the PII it is about to delete actually existed, so a fixture that stopped creating profiles cannot turn them into tests that delete nothing and assert nothing is absent. Verified load-bearing by mutation: weakening the FK to `ON DELETE SET NULL` and removing the ORM cascade fails four of the six. `known_limits` now states plainly what is still not covered, rather than letting the pass imply it: **INV-ARC-005 is not evidenced at all** — nothing gates PII in logs, telemetry, error messages, or URLs, and `DisplayMetadata` serializes decrypted names and the teacher note into the Flask session with no test on that boundary — the storage check is an AST scan that reads neither the live schema nor `migrations/**` and cannot prove a `*_hash` column is HMAC-produced rather than merely named so, and §VII.4 backup retention is outside CI entirely. `tests/test_pii_storage_validator.py` pins the validator in three directions (valid forms not flagged, real defects caught, and cannot pass by auditing nothing) and is declared under `CI-VALIDATION`. `CI-XDOMAIN` is now the only family with no evidence.
- **An attendance test failed for two hours out of every twenty-four (2026-09-05)** — `test_DOM_IDEN_001__enforce_daily_limits_taps_out_when_limit_reached_in_scope` seeded a session at `datetime.now(utc) - 2h` and asserted the tap-out landed at `started_at + limit`. `enforce_daily_limits_job` accrues against a *daily* limit and therefore clamps every interval with `max(active_start, day_start_utc)` — time spent yesterday does not count toward today's cap, which is correct. So whenever the suite runs within two hours of the class's evaluation-day boundary, the seed lands in *yesterday*, the job correctly taps out at `day_start + limit`, and the test fails for a reason unrelated to what it certifies. Production is right; the test was wall-clock fragile. All three seeds in the file now derive from the same `evaluation_day_boundaries` primitive the job uses, so the scenario they describe ("a session opened earlier today") is constructible at every hour rather than only at twenty-two of them. The assertion loses no strength: away from the boundary the seed is unchanged, and at the boundary it still verifies the limit arithmetic. Found because the identity suite happened to run just after local midnight — the same run passed in full ninety minutes earlier.
- **A support ticket from one student could reverse another student's transaction (2026-09-05)** — Regression introduced by this branch's own B7 fix and caught by the full suite, which is the reason it is written up rather than quietly squashed. B7 replaced `admin.resolve_issue`'s seat-ownership guard with a class-tenancy guard, on the reasoning that "what must be true before a teacher mutates a ledger row is that the row belongs to the class this teacher is acting in." That is true, and it was genuinely missing — but it is an *additional* condition, not a substitute. Two independent things must hold: **tenancy** (the row belongs to the acting class) and **binding** (the row belongs to the seat that submitted *this* issue). Dropping the second left same-class, different-seat reversals wide open: any student's ticket became a lever for reversing any classmate's transaction, and because DOM-SUP-001 defines `issue_resolution_actions` as a declaration log whose rows are append-only, the log would have permanently attributed that reversal to the wrong actor — a defect that survives the ledger correction. Both conditions are now asserted, and the seat lookup carries the `class_id` predicate B7 was right to demand (DOM-IDEN-001 §VI: `Seat.public_id` is only answerable with a class in hand). The same guard was applied to the sibling `compensating_transaction` branch, which moves money by the same mechanism and had the same gap. `tests/dom/interpretation/test_issue_resolution_reverse_transaction.py` — which certifies precisely the same-class/different-seat case, and which the B7 commit did not touch — was the only red test in a 1208-test run and is the reason this was found before merge.
- **Three CI workflows gated on a branch that does not exist, and the migration gate had never once run (2026-09-05)** — `actionlint.yml`, `check-migrations.yml`, and `policy-guardrails.yml` all filtered on `codex/v2.0`. That ref is absent both locally and on the remote (`git branch -a | grep -c codex/v2.0` returns `0`); the integration branch is `CTH_v2.0`. The consequences were not uniform, and the difference is the point. `actionlint` and `policy-guardrails` declare their `pull_request` leg without a branch filter, so both still ran on PRs and only their push legs were dead — but `policy-guardrails` additionally guards its `guardrails-main` job on `github.ref == 'refs/heads/codex/v2.0'`, a condition that can never be true, so the **strict `--no-waivers` check had never executed a single time**: waivers were accepted on every PR and never re-checked without them. `check-migrations` filtered *both* legs and was therefore wholly inert — no migration has ever been validated by CI, in a repository whose own documentation names migrations as the leading cause of deploy failure. All three now name `CTH_v2.0`, and `check-migrations` matches any target branch on the PR leg so that a gate of that consequence cannot go silently dead again the next time a branch is renamed. A second, independent defect in the same file: its `paths` filter watched `app/models/**`, but this repo has `app/models.py` — a module, not a package — so the filter would not have matched model changes even with the branch name corrected. Both spellings are now listed, matching the convention `schema-gate.yml` already used. Not fixed here, because it is a release decision rather than a defect: `deploy.yml` deploys production on push to `main`, and `main` is **1053 commits behind** `CTH_v2.0`, so promoting v2 requires an explicit choice between merging to `main` and repointing the trigger. Recorded in the tracker's new §V launch checklist.

- **Track T2 — the sysadmin escalation surface: a hard 500, a consent gap, a missing FEAT envelope, and unscoped seat resolution (2026-09-05)** — Four blockers, all found by source review rather than by a failing test, and all now pinned by `tests/dom/support/test_escalation_disclosure_and_scope.py`.
  - **B4 (500 on every sysadmin escalation view).** `_issue_to_view` in `app/routes/system_admin.py` read `issue.teacher.get_sysadmin_display_name()` — a relationship and a method that exist nowhere on `Issue`. The deeper cause was that `Issue.reviewer_public_id`, the column that exists precisely to record the reviewing teacher, **had no writer anywhere in the codebase**, so the view had no lawful source for the reviewer and reached for a fabricated one. The fix adds the writer: `admin.escalate_issue` now stamps `reviewer_public_id` with the teacher's class-scoped seat `public_id` (DOM-SUP-001 §VII, INV-ARC-019 §IX — sysadmins never see raw `seat_id`/`user_id`), and the three sysadmin templates render that instead of a name, falling back to `Unattributed`.
